28/11/25, 10:16 am - Kaiwal Panchal: Hey Saahil!

My apologies for being a little late in writing this email. As we discussed, you wanted to understand how my experience at my previous startup could be useful for us at Sylvr.

One of the key things I noticed while working there (and also from a close friend who still works there) was how simply the product started. The initial idea was to make TP schemes easily accessible to everyone using a Google Maps–like interface. At its core, the main problem they were solving was user experience. Over time, they did add more features that provided additional value, but the most important thing was that the website went live early. Improvements and new features were then driven largely by real customer usage and feedback.

If I try to draw parallels with Sylvr, I think our ideal approach should be similar: build something simple that directly targets the audience and gets people actively using Sylvr for a clear, immediate benefit. Once people start using the product, we can scale and add features based on what customers are actually asking for, combined with our own insights. A feedback loop like this tends to be far more rewarding than spending a long time building everything upfront before release.

For example, something as straightforward as allowing small businesses to analyse their balance sheets and compare them against broader market benchmarks could already be interesting enough for them to try Sylvr at least once. This could be a strong initial user acquisition strategy. Features like building a marketplace for consultants would be much easier to introduce once we can demonstrate that businesses are already onboarded and actively using the product. At that point, we can focus on building integrations and features that align well with the user base we’ve acquired.

I do like the current modules we’re focusing on, such as consumer feedback intelligence, the analyst agent, and the other frameworks we’ve planned (PESTEL, Porter’s Five Forces, etc.). I just feel that the starting point should be balance sheets and basic, actionable insights that deliver immediate value. The sooner we get this out, the better it is for us.

On the tech side, the TownPlanMap project was started by someone who was already the CTO of another company, and this was initially a side project for them, so most of the work happened under their supervision. That said, I do believe the current team we have is capable enough to handle what we’re building at Sylvr. A lot of the success there came from fast prototyping cycles and continuously shaping features based on user feedback.

Their tech stack was completely built on GCP, largely because Google provides generous credits to funded startups. Analytics, backend systems, and the use of Google’s Gemini models were all handled within that ecosystem. The web and mobile apps were initially built in Angular and later ported to React Native.

These are my overall observations from that experience, and I thought they might be useful context as we think about Sylvr’s product and execution strategy.

Sincerely,
Kaiwal Panchal
